# Decisions

Newest thinking is in the rationale, not the order. Change a decision by editing it here and saying why.

| # | Decision | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Node 24 runs TypeScript directly, with no build step | Matches `node --test` in the site, and means one less artefact. Needs a host on Node 24 (see QUESTIONS.md) |
| 2 | Plain `fetch` behind our own client, not jsforce | A library brings write methods we'd then have to prove unreachable. One send path is easy to audit and test |
| 3 | Allowlist by route and operation, not by HTTP method | GETs can run Apex, and POSTs can read. See ARCHITECTURE.md |
| 4 | `listMetadata`, `readMetadata` and `describeMetadata` are refused for now | Nothing uses them. Each read we add is a deliberate change with a test |
| 5 | No Bulk API yet | A bulk query is a POST that creates a job, so it widens the boundary. REST paging covers development orgs. Revisit for large orgs in Milestone 3 |
| 6 | Metadata retrieve over per-flow Tooling reads, when the connection can use the Metadata API | One retrieve costs a few calls. Tooling costs one call per flow, because `Metadata` is single-row. A read-only connection can't retrieve, so it falls back to Tooling (68) |
| 7 | Default budget stop at 50% of the org's daily limit, with a 5,000-call cap per run | The customer's integrations keep the larger share. Set by `--budget-share` or `HC_API_BUDGET_SHARE` |
| 8 | Unknown guards block exactly as failed guards do | A guard we couldn't run has told us nothing |
| 9 | Guards are enforced twice: engine and schema | A rule author can't bypass them by mistake |
| 10 | The Default Workflow User is a manual step | We couldn't confirm it's readable by API. Until it's answered, no licence removal is recommended. Confirm on the development org, and automate it if it can be read |
| 11 | Field removal is only recommended if no report uses the field, every report was read, and no private report is out of reach | Other people's private reports can't be read, so any that exist leave the guard unknown, and unknown blocks (rule 3). Most orgs have some, so field removal will usually stay "investigate". See QUESTIONS.md |
| 12 | Dashboard use is not measurable | `LastViewedDate` is per user. The obvious query would call a dashboard the whole team uses "unused" |
| 13 | No decision is separate from lost | Both win rates are reported, because deals that fizzle out would distort a single figure |
| 14 | A deal's close time comes from stage history, using its final closed run | Handles reopened deals, and CloseDate is often edited. CloseDate is the declared fallback |
| 15 | Conversion counts a skipped stage as passed, and reports skips separately | Otherwise a skipped stage shows as impossible conversion above 100% |
| 16 | Medians, not means, for time metrics | One stuck deal shouldn't move the headline |
| 17 | Multi-currency orgs get not measurable on value metrics | Adding amounts in mixed currencies is wrong. Supplying historic rates is future work. Counts still run |
| 18 | Days, months and quarters use the org's time zone, with UTC as a stated fallback | A close late on 31 March Pacific belongs in March. Instants are converted, and date-only fields such as CloseDate are left alone, because converting one would move it. The analysis window itself is still set in UTC in `run.ts`, so its edge can differ by a day. Every metric says which calendar it's on |
| 19 | Pseudonym tokens are assigned in user id order, per run | Stable within a run. Findings line up across runs by finding id, not token |
| 20 | One-word names aren't scrubbed from free text | "Sales" would turn Sales Cloud into a token. They're still tokenised wherever the engine labels a user |
| 21 | Fixtures are generated by running the real engine against a simulator | Hand-written JSON drifts. The simulator refuses SOQL it doesn't know, and replay refuses unrecorded requests |
| 22 | Ban-list tests aren't used for customer data | A list of customer names would itself put them in the repo. Tests require reserved domains and zero-padded ids instead |
| 23 | The CLI needs `HC_APPROVED_ORG_ALIASES`, empty by default | This machine has customer orgs logged in. One typo must not reach them |
| 24 | The CLI refuses to write inside a git work tree | Rule 8. Run folders hold CRM rows |
| 25 | Black Cloud's design system was readable at the artifact link | Colours and type match the brief. Tokens live in `src/design/tokens.ts`, checked against `colors_and_type.css`: brand colours match exactly, and muted text and hairlines use its neutrals. It has nothing for Carbon, so the two chart panel tints are ours |
| 26 | Recommended file storage: AWS S3 in `eu-west-2` (London) | See below |
| 27 | The fiscal year comes from the org unless the calibration sets it | A silent January default would give an April-start org the wrong quarters. A mismatch is raised as a finding, and if neither is readable, quarters aren't reported |
| 28 | Deals created already closed count in win rate, but not in conversion, sales cycle or slippage | They're real outcomes with no sales process behind them. Counting won ones as passing every stage while dropping lost ones inflated conversion |
| 29 | The Opportunity extract includes anything modified in the window, or in a calibrated open stage | Otherwise a bulk close of old deals vanishes, inflating win rate and undercounting past pipeline. `IsClosed` alone would let Salesforce decide what's open |
| 30 | A month end is judged on its own history gap (20%) and blank amounts (20%) | One old history row made months before a migration look measured |
| 32 | New reads for security: `SecurityHealthCheck` and `SecurityHealthCheckRisks` (Tooling), `PermissionSet`, `PermissionSetAssignment`, and `ApiType`, `Application` on `LoginHistory`, and sharing defaults on `Organization` | All are SOQL through existing routes, so the allowlist is unchanged. `OauthToken` isn't read, because it holds an authorisation code |
| 33 | New reads for current pipeline: `AccountId` on Opportunity, ids of open deals with `NextStep` filled, `ForecastingType`, `ForecastingQuota` | Text is read as filled or empty by filtering, so it's never fetched |
| 34 | New reads for forecast: `Period`, `ForecastingItem`, `ForecastingFact`. Forecast accuracy is not measurable | These rows are today's position, so they can't show what was called at the time |
| 35 | New reads for activity: `Task`, `Event`, `EmailMessage`, ids and dates only, for the last 365 days. `queryAll` stays off the allowlist | Archived activities need `queryAll`. A year of history is enough to start, and text fields are never selected |
| 36 | New reads for the lead funnel: `Lead`, `LeadHistory` (status changes, no values), `Campaign`, `CampaignMember`. Ids, dates, source and cost only | A funnel is counts and dates. Names, emails, phones, companies and campaign names are never selected |
| 37 | Every metric is not measurable when Opportunity can't be read, including ones that don't need it | `run.test.ts` pins this, and the rule against weakening tests decides it. It only matters when the pipeline report has already failed. See `docs/overnight/domains.md` |
| 38 | New read for revenue opportunities: `Account` id, owner and last activity, for accounts with a won deal | The name isn't read. Sized figures use only the org's own win rate, stated as a ceiling |
| 39 | New reads for report scanning: `Report` developer name and owner, `Folder`, a count of `Report USING SCOPE allPrivate`, and a Metadata retrieve of `Report` by name | The retrieve is an existing allowed operation, so the allowlist is unchanged. Only field names are kept: titles and filter values are dropped, because they can hold customer data |
| 40 | A domain's score is total earned over 8 times the rules counted. A pass earns 8, and an issue earns 8 minus its severity times its confidence factor | Each rule has a fixed maximum, so severity counts even with no passes. Earned over earned plus lost gave 0 to any domain with no passes, so five low issues scored the same as five critical ones |
| 41 | `LoginHistory`, `ForecastingItem` and `Campaign` are described before they're queried, in a `field-describe` step that checkpoints each describe. Optional fields the org lacks are dropped from the SELECT and listed in `missingFields` on the source, and only rules that need one are not measurable. A missing required field or object makes the source unavailable without a query. A failed describe falls back to the full query | A wrong field fails the whole query, so one feature-dependent field made every rule on that source not measurable. These three hold fields that depend on edition or features. Describe was already on the allowlist. Tooling objects can't be described this way, because Tooling describe isn't allowed |
| 42 | Field names follow the API 68.0 guides: Tooling `Layout` uses `EntityDefinitionId`, and `ApexTrigger` is read through REST, not Tooling | The Tooling reference doesn't list `TableEnumOrId` on `Layout`, or `Name`, `TableEnumOrId` and `NamespacePrefix` on `ApexTrigger`. The Object Reference lists all six trigger fields. See QUESTIONS.md |
| 43 | The 90-day plan holds 8, 10 and 10 actions, which is our working figure. Each action goes into the earliest month its severity and effort allow that still has room. Critical and high fixes that take up to medium effort can start in month one, and so can medium fixes that take very little. Large work starts in month three, or month two if it is critical. Low-severity work waits until month three. Whatever doesn't fit is listed after 90 days | Phasing by effort alone put 47 of 66 sample actions into the first month. Coby asked for my recommendation on 3 Nov |
| 44 | A domain gets a score only when at least 2 checks, and at least half its checks, could be measured | On the first live run, one calibration check scored historic pipeline 100 while every pipeline metric was not measurable. A score that rests on almost nothing misleads, so the domain says why it has no score instead (rule 5) |
| 45 | `hc benchmark` adds a run to the benchmark set only with `--consent`, once per run, recorded in the run folder as `benchmark-consent.json`. Rows are built from a fixed allowlist in `src/benchmark/export.ts`: measured domain scores, org-wide pipeline, activity and lead rates and days from `metrics.json`, and org-wide rates that data quality, best practice, utilisation and security checks record on a pass and an issue alike. Segments, series, stats, counts and amounts are never read. Industry comes from a fixed list of nine. The user band counts active standard users, leaving out accounts calibrated as shared, system, integration or test. The deal size band uses the median amount of won deals closed in the window, with no amount or zero left out, and is refused for multi-currency orgs or below `minSample` won deals. The period is the calendar quarter of the window end. Both bands are read from the raw extracts, so a purged run can't be exported | Rule 11. A row must not lead back to a customer, so it carries no run id, date or path, and the consent sits with the customer's own data instead. A figure only some orgs record (say, on an issue) would skew the set. Calendar quarters line up across orgs where fiscal ones don't. The bands aren't analysis outputs, and we'd rather refuse than guess (rule 5) |
| 46 | `hc purge` deletes a run's `checkpoints/` folder and nothing else, then writes `purged.json`. It refuses a folder without a valid `run.json`, and checkpoints that are a link. `--older-than` sweeps a runs folder by run date (`asOf`), skipping links and runs already purged | Raw extracts go 30 days after delivery. Findings, metrics, scores, calibration and the pseudonym map stay, so `hc render` still works: it never reads checkpoints. Delivery isn't recorded yet, and the run date comes first, so purging by it is never later than purging by delivery |
| 31 | The instance URL is parsed, and must be a bare https Salesforce host, on connect and refresh | A prefix check passes `https://x.salesforce.com@evil.example`, which sends the token to evil.example |

## File storage

We recommend **AWS S3 in eu-west-2 (London)**, with default KMS encryption, Block Public Access on, and a lifecycle rule that deletes extracts 30 days after delivery.

- **Region:** London keeps data in the UK, which is the simplest story for a UK customer's security review.
- **Fits Vercel:** the browser uploads screenshots straight to S3 through a presigned URL, so the file never passes through a function's request size limit. The worker reads with its own credentials.
- **Deletion:** a lifecycle rule enforces the retention promise even if our own code fails.

Alternatives considered:

- **Vercel Blob:** private stores and region choice exist, but we couldn't confirm a London store, and the region can't be changed afterwards. Reads also go through a function.
- **Cloudflare R2 with EU jurisdiction:** a good fallback if AWS isn't wanted, but it's EU rather than UK.

## Connecting customer orgs

**Answer: yes, customer orgs need the External Client App installed from a managed package (2GP).** Checked on 2026-11-03 against Salesforce's help and guides. It hasn't been tried from a second org, because this work could only touch `bc-hc-dev` (QUESTIONS.md).

- **A local app only works in its own org.** "Local external client apps are available only on the org where you create them." ([Create an External Client App](https://help.salesforce.com/s/articleView?id=xcloud.create_a_local_external_client_app.htm&language=en_US&type=5)). "External client apps can't be used until they're installed in an org, unlike connected apps that can be used by any org without installation" (Canvas Developer Guide, API 68.0, `canvas_framework.pdf`). "Only packaged apps can be deployed to other orgs" ([Comparison of Connected Apps and External Client Apps](https://help.salesforce.com/s/articleView?id=xcloud.connected_apps_and_external_client_apps_features.htm&language=en_US&type=5)). Even the Salesforce CLI says it "requires an external client app in the org that you're authorizing" (SFDX Developer Guide, API 68.0).
- **Packaging means 2GP.** "Packageable apps are packaged with second-generation (2GP) managed packaging and distributed to subscriber orgs" ([External Client Apps](https://help.salesforce.com/s/articleView?id=xcloud.external_client_apps.htm&language=en_US&type=5)). 1GP is also allowed, and 2GP with a Dev Hub is recommended ([Configure Packageable External Client Apps](https://help.salesforce.com/s/articleView?id=xcloud.configure_packageable_external_client_apps.htm&language=en_US&type=5)).
- **The connected app route is closing.** Since early September 2025, a required, automatic restriction blocks new users from uninstalled connected apps, unless they hold Approve Uninstalled Connected Apps, which only the standard System Administrator profile gets automatically ([knowledge article 005132365](https://help.salesforce.com/apex/HTViewSolution?id=005132365&language=en_US)). A read-only integration user wouldn't hold it, so each customer would have to install the app from Connected Apps OAuth Usage anyway. From Spring '26, new connected apps can only be created through Salesforce Support, and "it is the ISV's responsibility to provide a migrated ECA version of their app" ([knowledge article 005228017](https://help.salesforce.com/apex/HTViewSolution?id=005228017&language=en_US)).

What it takes:

- A namespace, a Dev Hub with second-generation packaging switched on (it can't be switched off), and a managed package holding the app and Barometer Read Only. A version must be promoted before it installs in production; beta versions install only in scratch orgs and sandboxes (2GP guide, API 68.0).
- The Dev Hub should be a production or partner business org. The 2GP guide warns that if a package's Dev Hub "expires or becomes inactive, the installed package can't be updated", and Developer Edition orgs expire with inactivity. ISVs are told to define packaged apps in their partner business org. `bc-hc-dev` is fine for development, not for the package customers install.
- No security review is needed for a private, unlisted package. Customers see a notice at install that it hasn't been through one.
- Deleting the app in the source org breaks it in every customer org, because they depend on its global OAuth settings.
- The customer's admin controls the policies, which are never packaged: permitted users, IP relaxation, refresh token expiry, session timeout. Our install instructions should ask for "admin approved users are pre-authorised" with Barometer Read Only, so nobody else, administrators included, can authorise the app.

Still open: whether an API-only integration user can complete the interactive consent (QUESTIONS.md). If it can't, customers either use a full-licence user with only Barometer Read Only, or pre-authorise the integration user for JWT bearer, as `bc-hc-dev` does.

## New environment variables

Nothing here is needed to run the tests.

| Variable | Needed | Purpose |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `HC_APPROVED_ORG_ALIASES` | For `hc run` and `hc connect` | Comma-separated Salesforce CLI aliases and OAuth connection names this machine may use |
| `HC_API_BUDGET_SHARE` | Optional | Share of the daily limit to stop at. Default 0.5 |
| `HC_SF_API_VERSION` | Optional | Pins an API version. Default is the org's newest |
| `HC_TOKEN_KEY` | The worker, or any machine that isn't a Mac | Base64 32-byte AES key for stored tokens. On a Mac, the keychain is used when it's unset |
| `HC_SF_CLIENT_ID` | Optional | The External Client App's consumer key. Otherwise read from `~/.barometer/oauth-client.json` (mode 600) |
| `HC_SF_CLIENT_SECRET` | With `HC_SF_CLIENT_ID` | Its consumer secret. Required by the app for the code exchange and refresh |
| `HC_SF_REDIRECT_URI` | With `HC_SF_CLIENT_ID` | The callback registered on the app |
| `HC_CONNECTIONS_DIR` | Optional | Where encrypted connections are stored. Default `~/.barometer/connections` |
| `HC_CLEANUP_DRY_RUN` | Optional, the worker | `1` makes the daily retention sweep log what it would delete and delete nothing. `0` or unset: it deletes (98) |
| `HC_DEV_JWT_USERNAME` | Optional, development | The user `scripts/deploy-dev-jwt-app.sh` records for JWT bearer. Default the `bc-hc-dev` read-only user |

Expected later: `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, `REDIS_URL`, `DATABASE_URL` and object storage credentials.

## Report decisions

Kept apart from the table above so the overnight branches merge cleanly.

| # | Decision | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| R1 | The number check matches value and unit, and blocks whatever it can't read | An invented figure is the worst failure this product has. A false alarm only costs a redraft |
| R2 | A rounded figure must round from the real one at its written precision, and stay within 5% | "£2.9m" for 2,892,000 is honest. "£1m" for 1.4m rounds correctly and still misleads |
| R3 | "half" and "a third" must be within one point of a real percentage, with no allowance for "nearly" | Hedging words are how a loose figure gets through |
| R4 | The check can't prove a true number sits on the right claim, so each section is checked against its own sources only | It narrows the risk. Consultant approval covers the rest |
| R5 | A draft paragraph must cite its sources, and its figures are checked against those sources only | A figure from elsewhere in the run is true but unsupported where it's used |
| R6 | One redraft with the failures listed, then the section keeps the engine's text | Repeated retries cost money and rarely help. A person is better value |
| R7 | Server-side refusal fallbacks are off | They'd move customer metadata to another model with nobody deciding, while zero data retention is still an open question (QUESTIONS.md). A refused section keeps the engine's text. Worth revisiting once that's answered |
| R8 | First and last names are masked in prompts as well, apart from a short list of ordinary words | `scrub()` catches full names only, and metadata labels such as "Sam's pipeline" hold first names |
| R9 | Evidence isn't sent to Claude | It isn't needed for wording, and a query can quote record values |
| R10 | Charts sit on a Carbon panel | Chartreuse is 1.5:1 on Ivory, which fails the chart contrast check. It's about 10:1 on Carbon |
| R11 | Poppins is embedded in PDFs from `@fontsource/poppins` (OFL), and only named in Word | PDF rendering mustn't need the network. A machine without Poppins shows Word's fallback font |
| R12 | A domain nobody assessed shows no issue count | "0 issues" would read as a clean result |
| R13 | Chromium only runs with the network off, and with routes other than `data:` aborted | A report holds customer data, and rendering has no reason to load or send anything |
| R14 | The number check runs again on printed page text, against the whole run | It catches anything a template adds after section checks, including chart labels. It's unscoped, so it's weaker than the section check |
| R15 | Deck density flexes in steps: more cards, then two columns, then one summary slide in place of domain openers. Tables are the last resort | 40 to 60 slides has to hold for a thin org and a huge one. Every step before the last keeps each issue's summary and recommendation. Only the last cuts anything, and it says so |
| R16 | A deck under 40 slides is flagged, never padded | Filler slides would be worse than a short report. The layout check reports it, and a consultant decides |
| R17 | Reports show overridden figures. Raw notes show the original with the override beside it | An executive reader shouldn't see a corrected number. The audit trail needs both |
| R18 | The pipeline deep dive adds open pipeline by stage, the delivery outlook, quarterly trends, where wins come from, where deals are lost, and close dates with stage speed. Sales cycle and slippage share a slide. In two-column densities, the deep dive pairs charts, and metric definitions and the after-90-days list go into columns | The deep dive took the deck past 60 for an ordinary org. Pairing keeps every chart, and the lists are reference. An ordinary org may now reach the domain summary step (R15), which still keeps every issue's summary and recommendation |
| R19 | The executive summary has a pipeline page after the scorecard: pipeline today, weighted value, expected value by month, and one line of potential revenue for each sizing rule, never totalled. The 90-day plan pages itself by measured line heights: later phases move to another page, and only when the plan wouldn't fit the pages left does it drop actions, from the longest phase first, each phase keeping at least one and the note that the full report has the rest | Long recommendations ran the plan into the footer. Moving a phase is better than cutting one, and the 8-page limit still holds |
| R20 | `compactMoney` rounds half up in decimal | `toPrecision` rounds the binary value, so £3,255,000 printed as £3.25m and failed the number check, which rounds half up |

| 47 | An org goes into the benchmark set at most once in any 4 quarters: `hc benchmark` refuses a run when another run of the same org in the same runs folder was exported within that window. `hc benchmark --summary` counts only rows in the 4 quarters ending at the newest period | The minimum group of 10 means 10 orgs, but rows can't be traced to an org, so they can't be de-duplicated afterwards. Refusing at export, where the org id is still known, and windowing the count means a repeat check a year on replaces an org's figures rather than adding a second org |
| 48 | The customer's name comes from the org's company information (`Organization.Name`) unless a run or intake gives one. It goes on the report covers and is registered with the pseudonymiser as a `customer` token, so it's masked before anything reaches Claude | Coby asked for the company information to be the name by default. The cover needs a name, and masking it keeps rule 7 for the customer as well as for people |
| 49 | Headcount bands are LinkedIn's company sizes (1-10, 11-50, 51-200, 201-500, 501-1000, 1001-5000, 5001+), and the intake records the LinkedIn URL, industry and headcount band. `hc benchmark` reads industry and headcount from the intake, and the flags only override it | Consultants read headcount from LinkedIn, so its bands avoid a conversion that can't be done (11-50 straddles 49 and 50). The URL is kept as the source. We don't scrape LinkedIn: it needs a login and its terms forbid it |
| 50 | Stale, past-close, ownerless and quiet open deals are sized as potential revenue: each deal's Amount at the share of comparable deals that still won, from `potentialRevenue` in `historic-pipeline/potential.ts`. Comparable means deals closed in the window, rebuilt from stage history, that were in the same situation while open: past the close date they had then (`dq.open-opps-past-close`), in one stage for 30 or 60 days (`cur.stale-deals`, `rev.stalled-deals`), with no change of any kind for 90 days (`dq.open-opps-no-activity`), or in the same stage (`dq.opps-inactive-owner`). Won is counted against won, lost and no decision. Below `minSample` comparable deals, a stale situation falls back to the org's own win rate, called a ceiling, and a stage isn't sized. The raw exposure, the deals with no amount and the period are stated, and no amount is imputed | The customer review: old opportunities are still potential opportunities. Exposure alone overstated them, and the average win rate flattered them. Past activity isn't in stage history, so no history row for 90 days stands in for no activity, and says so. When an owner left isn't in stage history either, so ownerless deals are compared by stage. A late stage can win more often than the average deal, so the overall rate is no ceiling there (rule 5). The five findings overlap, so they're never added together |
| 51 | The delivery outlook weights each open deal by its stage's win rate (DECISIONS 50's cohort, by stage) and places it in the month it's expected to close: its current close date, or its first close date moved by the median days won deals closed after their first date, whichever is later. A deal whose expected date has passed has no month and is shown apart. It covers this month and the 5 after it. Stages with too few closed deals aren't weighted, and too few won deals with history makes the outlook not measurable | It's for whoever staffs the work once it's won, so it's an expectation by month rather than a forecast by rep. Moving the first date by the median lateness, without going earlier than today's date, doesn't double-count a deal that's already been pushed. Overdue deals have no honest month, so they aren't spread across the next ones |
| 52 | New read: Opportunity `Id`, `AccountId`, `CloseDate` for wins before the analysis window (`prior-wins`). A won deal is from a repeat customer when its account had already won, before the window or earlier in it. Won means `IsWon` or a stage the calibration calls won. If the read fails, new and repeat are not measurable | Without earlier wins, every account's first win in the window would read as new, which is exactly wrong for a business built on repeat clients. Ids and dates only, SOQL through the existing route, so the allowlist is unchanged |
| 53 | A stage's win rate counts closed deals that were in that stage at some point, not those that reached it or later. Today's open deals by stage use a minimum of 10 deals for a stage's median age, not `minSample`. Top accounts are shown as cumulative shares, with accounts as tokens | "Were in it" handles retired and renamed stages without guessing where they sit. Today's open deals are the whole population, not a sample, so a smaller group is still exact, and 10 is where one deal stops moving it far. An account's name is never read |
| 54 | Two documentation checks: `aut.undocumented-automation` (active flows, Process Builders, Workflow Rules and validation rules with no description, and active non-test Apex with no comment at all) and `cfg.undocumented-fields` (own custom fields with neither help text nor a description). Each is an issue past 50% undocumented; automation also fires when one type is over 80%. Whether code has a comment and whether it's a test are worked out at extraction, so bodies still aren't kept | A real customer's main complaint was an org nobody new could use or manage, with no record of what its automation does. Descriptions and comments are the only places Salesforce keeps that, so their absence is measurable. The thresholds are our judgement |
| 55 | An object inventory, `configuration.object_inventory`, counts for each core object, and each custom object of your own with records: custom fields, active record types, page layouts, and the active validation rules, record-triggered flows, Process Builders, Workflow Rules and Apex triggers on it, with how many of those have no description (no comment, for a trigger). Core objects are counted from their describe, custom objects from the retrieve, which now keeps record types and the list of objects defined. One new read: Tooling `SELECT Id, DeveloperName, NamespacePrefix FROM CustomObject`, matched on the first 15 characters, with names rebuilt as `<prefix>__<DeveloperName>__c`. Two ids that would make the same name are both left unnamed. Objects are ranked by automation, then custom fields. The full report shows the busiest 10 in the configuration section, and the raw notes list every object | The first customer's head of delivery said nobody new could find their way round the org. The counts were already read, except for one link: `Layout` and `ApexTrigger` name a custom object by its id, and nothing else we read maps that to a name. Packaged objects are left out, because their automation isn't the customer's to maintain. A count we couldn't read is shown as not read, never zero (rule 5). It follows DECISIONS 37, so it's not measurable when Opportunity can't be read |
| 56 | `aut.mixed-automation-on-object` and `aut.multiple-triggers` (both version 2) group triggers by object name, through the resolver the inventory uses (`src/domains/object-names.ts`). A trigger on a custom object we can't name is never shown by its id. Mixed automation leaves it out of every object, because it can't be matched to a flow or rule, and says how many. Multiple triggers still groups it by its id, cut to 15 characters, because one id is one object, and calls it "a custom object we couldn't name" | Keyed by raw `TableEnumOrId`, a custom object's trigger and its flow counted as two objects, so mixed automation missed it, and two triggers by its 15 and 18-character ids looked like two objects. An id in a finding means nothing to a reader |
| 57 | The OAuth token and revoke endpoints (`/services/oauth2/token`, `/services/oauth2/revoke`) are the one way out besides the client. They're in `transport.ts`, as `fetchOAuthTransport`, behind `assertOAuthEndpoint`: https, a Salesforce host, exactly those two paths, no query string. `oauth.ts` checks every endpoint again before calling any transport, fakes included. Source scan tests pin one POST in `transport.ts` and two checked calls in `oauth.ts` | Getting and refreshing a token are POSTs outside the data API, so the client's allowlist can't cover them, and they carry codes, refresh tokens and the client secret. Keeping them in the one network file, with their own narrow check, keeps the boundary auditable. The client still has no generic request method |
| 58 | The Barometer External Client App is source metadata in `salesforce/force-app` and `salesforce/source-org` (see 66), deployed with the CLI: web server flow, PKCE required, client secret required for the code exchange and refresh, refresh token rotation on, scopes `api` and `refresh_token` only, refresh tokens expire after 30 days unused, IP restrictions enforced, every other flow off. Callback `http://localhost:8787/oauth/callback` for development. Permitted users was "all users may self-authorise" in `bc-hc-dev`, and is admin approved with nobody approved in BlackCloudProd; customers should set "admin approved users are pre-authorised" with Barometer Read Only (see 61) | Fields come from the org's own Metadata WSDL (API 68.0) and were checked by deploying and retrieving. | 100 | Adoption rules. Falling adoption (`adp.falling-adoption`): monthly active users in the latest full month 20% or more below the peak of the months at least 3 months earlier, for the org (high) or for any profile or role of 5 or more whose peak was 5 or more (medium); it needs 4 whole months of login history, or it's not measurable. Teams that barely log in (`adp.low-engagement-group`): a profile or role of 5 or more where over half logged in on fewer than 4 of the last 28 days (medium). Users still on Classic (`adp.classic-users`): active people whose Lightning preference is false, low, or medium above 25%. All three are not sized. Reports nobody runs stay with `utl.unused-reports`, which already covers them, and no adoption rule repeats it. No value group was added, and `src/value` is unchanged | A seat nobody uses is already counted as licence waste, so a figure for low adoption would count it twice. Confidence on falling adoption is medium for the same reason |

| 114 | Sales process rules, all not sized, with no value group: they say how far the stages can be trusted, which has no honest figure. Won deals that skip a stage (`pipe.skipped-stages`): more than 10% of won deals skipped an expected stage, or more than 25% in a segment of the calibrated minimum (medium; high confidence when the calibration names the stages, medium when they're inferred). Deals that go back a stage (`pipe.going-backwards`): more than 15% of closed deals, or more than 30% in a segment (medium). A stage where lost deals wait before they die (`pipe.dead-end-stage`): the stage lost and no decision deals leave from most, holding at least a third of them, where their median time in it is longer than three quarters of won deals took there (medium, recommendationType change). It isn't `historic.lost_by_stage`, which only counts where lost deals leave from and raises nothing, nor `cur.stuck-in-stage`, which is about open deals, so it reuses their parts (`stageLeftFrom`, `daysByStage`, and the same 75th percentile line) rather than changing either. Skipping is held to a tighter limit than going back, because a won deal that never entered a stage the process needs is a gap in the record, while a deal stepping back can be honest. A third, rather than most, because with four or five open stages a stage holding a third of losses already stands out |

Win rate is won deals as a share of won and lost deals, exactly as `historic.win_rate` defines it: no decision deals are counted, set aside and listed in the exclusions. Each factor is two metric segments, dimension the factor's label and key `with` or `without`, n the deals on that side and value its win rate. The gap in points, the wins on each side and the segment check are stats (`backwardsGap`, `backwardsWithWon`, `backwardsCompared` and so on). The metric's value is the win rate of all these deals. A factor with fewer than `calibration.minSample` deals on either side is not measurable, with that reason on both its segments, and when no factor can be compared the whole metric is not measurable, with each factor's reason. `buildReport` adds each gap to the dataset as a points figure under the metric, because a stat is a plain number and can't back "61.9 points" | The brief asked for won out of won, lost and no decision, and also to match `historic.win_rate` exactly. | 161 | The five factors, all read from what the engine already holds for each deal. Had logged activity: `LastActivityDate` is set. Three checks move from automation with their ids and versions unchanged: `aut.apex-coverage`, `aut.old-api-versions` and `aut.multiple-triggers`. Automation keeps how the automation is built: Workflow Rules, Process Builder, mixed tools on an object, hardcoded ids, fault paths and undocumented automation. A test data factory, with no test methods, isn't counted. It's medium when 20% or more of test classes assert nothing, otherwise low. `tech.governor-limit-risk`: a query (`[SELECT`, `Database.query`) or a save (a DML statement, `Database.insert` and the like) inside a loop's body, in active code that isn't a test. A SOQL for loop's own query doesn't count. It's high when a trigger does it. `tech.apex-code-size`: `LengthWithoutComments` of active classes not marked `@isTest`, and triggers, against 6,000,000 characters. Medium at 75%, high at 90%. `tech.classic-leftovers`: JavaScript buttons and links (`WebLink.LinkType`), s-controls, and Visualforce pages with `IsAvailableInTouch` false, packages' own left out. Medium when there's a JavaScript button or s-control, low for pages alone. People still working in Classic stay with adoption (`adp.classic-users`) | A finding's id hangs on its rule id and key only (`findingId`), never the domain, so a re-check lines a moved check up with its baseline. The 6 MB limit and test classes not counting against it are from the Apex Developer Guide. The 100 queries and 150 saves are its per-transaction limits for synchronous Apex |

The same sheets always make the same bytes | We need a tenth of what a spreadsheet library does: text, numbers, a bold frozen header with a filter, widths, and links. The popular libraries are large or have a troubled release history, and each package added is one more to trust in a worker that holds CRM data. Writing text as text means a record named "=SUM(...)" stays a name. The sample was opened with openpyxl 3.1.5 in a scratch environment, not with Excel or Google Sheets (QUESTIONS.md) |
| 192 | Findings carry no records: rule 7 keeps them out, and a record check holds only its segments. So a rule hands the records it judged to the engine as `records` on its issue (`RecordList` in `src/engine/rule.ts`): ids, what's wrong in words that hold no name, and the fix. The engine keeps them out of the finding, scrubs the words but never the ids (the scrub would turn a user's id into their token), and keeps only those whose finding ended as an issue. Fifteen checks hand them over: `dq.open-opps-past-close`, `dq.opps-inactive-owner`, `dq.open-opps-missing-amount`, `dq.open-opps-no-activity`, `dq.loss-reason-missing`, `dq.stale-leads`, `dq.contact-completeness`, `cur.stuck-in-stage`, `cur.stale-deals`, `cur.close-date-pushes`, `cur.missing-next-steps`, `utl.dormant-licensed-users`, `utl.never-logged-in`, `bp.users-without-role` and `adp.classic-users`. A set-up issue (fields, flows, classes) has no sheet: its items are named in its ticket and in the raw notes | The same code that decides a deal is a hit lists it, so the sheet can't disagree with the finding: on the synthetic org every deal list's length equals its finding's `hits`. The two shared deal builders (`dealRule`, `shareRule`) carry it for nine checks. Set-up items are read by name without ids, so they can't be linked |
| 193 | Deal and user lists are every record, because the checks already judge every row of the opportunity read (with its existing 200,000 ceiling) and of the users read. Opportunity names come in that same read, with no extra call, kept apart from the rows as `names`, which no rule reads. Stale leads and incomplete contacts were only counted, so a new resumable step, `action-records`, reads them with the counts' own filters (`staleLeadFilter`, `AccountId = null`, `Email = null`), selecting the id, name and owner only. It reads at most 5,000 rows a list (`ACTION_ROWS`), keyset by id. A list that stops there says "the first N of M, in order of Salesforce id", with how to see the rest in a list view. Contacts get a line per gap, so one with neither an account nor an email appears twice | Within the budget (rule 9): three small lists, each a few pages at most. On the synthetic org the recording grew by three exchanges, from 130 to 133. Reading every lead in a large org could cost hundreds of calls for a list nobody works through row by row, so a ceiling with an honest label is better than a budget stop. The step is a record extract, so a set-up review never runs it |
| 194 | People follow roadmap decision 11, read from `namedRepConsent` at intake. With consent, a record's owner and a user who is the record are named. Without it, an owner is given by role ("No role" when there's none), a user by profile and role, and every text goes through `teamLevelText`. The column headers say which ("Owner" or "Owner's role"), and the front sheet says it in words. Deal, lead and contact names are always shown, since exports are where named records belong (roadmap decision 10). Every record keeps its id and link either way | The customer's own admin can open any record from its link, so leaving a name out never stops the fix. A user record is a person, so it follows the same rule as an owner |
| 195 | The ticket file is CSV with these columns: Summary, Issue Type ("Task"), Priority, Description, Labels, Labels, Labels, Effort, Owner role, Reference, Records. Priority comes from severity: critical is Highest, high is High, medium is Medium, low is Low, and info is Lowest. The three labels are "barometer", the area's id and the rule's id. The description holds the summary, what to do, the records or items, the effort and owner, and where the working is. It's RFC 4180, every field quoted, CRLF line ends, UTF-8 without a byte order mark. A field that starts with `=`, `+`, `-`, `@`, a tab or a carriage return gets a leading apostrophe. There's one row an issue, in reference order | These are Jira's default priority names, and its CSV importer takes a repeated "Labels" column as several labels. Labels can't hold spaces, and ids have none. Effort is a size, not hours, because we don't hold an estimate, so it's a column to map or ignore rather than Original Estimate. The apostrophe stops a spreadsheet running a field as a formula. Without a byte order mark the header reads as "Summary" in importers that don't strip one |
| 196 | Each run keeps its lists as `action-lists.json` in the run folder, private to the worker (0600), beside `pseudonym-map.json` and outside `checkpoints/`. The customer's microsite doesn't link them: nothing records whether, or with whom, the client's Drive folder is shared, so a link could only open for some readers, and would ask the rest to request access to Black Cloud's drive `hc render` writes them too, for a draft. Permissions go to security, packages to utilisation, storage to technical health and the data model to data quality. Territory and quotas get no new check (206). An area of one or two checks either can't be scored (`MIN_RULES_SCORED`) or swings on a single finding, and each new one thins the weight of the rest. Each new check answers the question its host area already asks: who can do what, what you pay for, what holds the org up, and whether the data can be trusted |
| 202 | Permissions: two checks in security. `sec.unused-access`: custom profiles for internal users with no active users, and the org's own permission sets held by nobody, directly or through an assigned permission set group. Low severity, to investigate, since deleting one is destructive. `sec.peer-outlier-access`: in a profile with 5 or more active people, other than administrators, a permission set of the org's own that exactly one of them holds. Low severity and medium confidence, to investigate, with the people in the action lists. Sets granting Modify or View All Data are left to their own checks. Both read `access`, a new set-up step: profiles' own sets with their user type, every active user's assignments (to a set or a group), and `PermissionSetGroupComponent`. "Your org, documented" now counts a set's holders the same way (`holdersOf`), so it no longer shows a set held through a group as held by nobody | A set in an assigned group is in use, so counting direct assignments alone would call it unused: on the synthetic org, Report Builder showed 0 holders in "Your org, documented" until this change. The users read holds internal users only, so a portal or community profile would always look empty, and it's left out. One person's extra access is often right, for a team lead or a temporary job, which is why it's something to check. The 5 is our judgement. No allowlist change: these are ordinary queries |
| 203 | Packages: `utl.package-seats` in utilisation. Of the installed apps sold by the seat, it finds those with nobody assigned (medium) and those with at least 2 seats, and 10% or more, unassigned (low, the same limits as user licences). Site licences, and free, trial and expired apps, are left out. Apps are named by namespace, because the licence record holds no app name. A figure comes only from the calibration's price for a seat, keyed `package:<namespace>` in `licenceCosts`, and is counted as licence waste. Overlapping apps aren't judged | Nothing in the set-up says two apps do the same job, so overlap would be a guess, and Claude may not introduce one either. List prices for third-party apps aren't public in a form we hold. On the synthetic org, acmecpq has 8 of 20 seats spare |
| 204 | Storage: `tech.storage-use` in technical health. Data and file storage come from `/limits` (`DataStorageMB`, `FileStorageMB`), added to the limits the client keeps on connecting, so there's no new request. Medium at 80% used, high at 95%, both our judgement, and the summary names the five objects with the most records. Growth isn't read, and storage has no figure | The org keeps no history of its storage, and a rate worked out from record creation dates would ignore what was deleted, so any growth rate would be an estimate (rule 5). We hold no list price for extra storage, so the cost of growth is left out, as the brief asked. Technical health already holds the Apex size limit, so the org's limits sit together |
| 205 | Data model: two checks in data quality. `dq.opps-without-account`: open deals with no account, through the shared deal rule (over 2% of open deals, or 20% in a segment), each one in the action lists. `dq.duplicate-accounts`: accounts sharing an exact name, from one aggregate query (`GROUP BY Name HAVING COUNT(Id) > 1`, the 200 largest groups), then the accounts in those groups read by name, up to 5,000 rows. Low severity, or medium when 5% or more of accounts are in a group (our judgement), at medium confidence, and to investigate rather than merge. Names stay in the worker: the finding counts, and only the action list shows them. Not built: field fill rates, which `cfg.unused-custom-fields` already measures per object; record-type sprawl, since judging use needs a count per record type on every object, and "Your org, documented" already lists active types; and orphaned contacts, which `dq.contact-completeness` covers | Exact names understate duplication ("Acme Ltd" and "Acme Limited" aren't matched), which is the safe side. | 216 | Salesforce CPQ (roadmap Stage 6) is a module the consultant switches on at intake, `cpq`, off by default, as Service Cloud is (156). It brings two scored areas, `cpq_quoting` and `cpq_setup` (`src/domains/cpq`). It's for Salesforce CPQ, the SBQQ package. CPQ counts as installed when its package has a licence record or its quote object can be described. When it isn't, every CPQ check is not measurable with "Salesforce CPQ (the SBQQ package) isn't installed", after one describe and no query of its objects. When we can't tell, every check says why. Revenue Cloud Advanced and Agentforce Revenue Management aren't built | A free package has no licence record, so a missing record alone isn't proof, and the describe settles it for one call. A 20 to 500 user UK firm on CPQ loses most to discounting it can't see, quotes that don't reach finance, and renewals nobody started, so those lead. We couldn't verify Revenue Cloud Advanced's object names, so it's in QUESTIONS.md rather than guessed |
| 217 | CPQ set-up has three checks. `cpq.price-rule-conflicts`: active price rules whose actions set the same field, at an overlapping evaluation event and the same evaluation order (medium severity and confidence). `cpq.twin-field-mismatch`: a custom field on quote lines sharing its API name with a field of another type on the opportunity product or order product (medium). `cpq.inactive-rules`: three or more inactive price and product rules making up 20% or more of them (low, to investigate). CPQ keeps its set-up as records of its own objects, so a set-up review reads price and product rules as configuration, and never a quote or contract. The set-up review test now says so explicitly | Trailhead says CPQ orders rules by evaluation event, then evaluation order, and that twin fields copy only when the API name and data type match exactly. What happens on a tie in order isn't stated, so a tie is a risk to check, at medium confidence: two rules' conditions may never both be true, which code can't tell. Twin fields are compared from describes, which hold names and types and no data |
| 218 | Discount leakage, `cpq.discount-leakage`, is the money story. It reads primary quotes on deals won or lost in the window, each discount being (list amount − net amount) / list amount. It compares like with like: a team against the other teams, and a rep against their own team-mates. Each side needs the calibration's sample of won deals (20). A segment stands out when its median discount on won deals is 10 points or more deeper than its peers' (our judgement), and it wins no more often. Its figure is each won deal's discount past its peers' median, applied to its list amount, each deal once. It's labelled potential, because it assumes those deals would still have been won, and the value model divides it by the window's years. The summary also gives the whole org's win rate above and below the median discount, saying that this mixes markets. Reps appear as tokens, named only with consent (194) | Comparing with the whole org failed on the synthetic data, and would on real orgs: a team that sells into a harder market loses more and discounts differently, and drags the org figure. Team-mates sell into the same market. Every figure is read from the org's own quotes and outcomes; the only assumption is the counterfactual, which the finding names (rule 5). On the synthetic org, one rep discounts 30% against 9% for their team-mate and wins 56% of closed deals against 60.3%, which is £835,472 over two years |
| 219 | Three more quoting checks. `cpq.discount-approvals`: primary quotes discounted 20% or more off list (our judgement, said in the finding) with no approval process in Salesforce. Approvals are matched to quotes by id, from `ProcessInstance` started in the window. It's not measurable when Advanced Approvals (sbaa) is installed, since we don't read its records. `cpq.quote-to-order`: won deals whose primary quote was never ordered. It's not measurable when no won quote was ordered at all, since orders then aren't how the org closes. `cpq.renewals-not-started`: activated contracts ending in the next 120 days with no renewal opportunity (high severity) | Matching by id avoids a filter on the polymorphic `TargetObject`. Approval can happen outside Salesforce, so the approvals check is medium confidence. A renewal missing from the pipeline is revenue nobody is forecasting, so it's high severity |

CPQ is in `ALL_MODULES`, so the fixtures record it, and `knownModuleIds` and `modulesForRun` know "cpq". `hc run --cpq` and `npm run sample -- --cpq` add it | What a quote is sold for is revenue itself, as case handling is for Service Cloud, and the set-up is a step further back. | 236 | Readiness for Data Cloud and Agentforce (roadmap Stage 6) answers one question: could this org put an AI agent to work safely, and what would stop it? Each is parsed from whichever file holds its type, and one that doesn't come back is unknown, never off. | 239 | How each pillar is decided, by code. Data and access re-read whole areas: data quality for data; security, plus three integration checks, for access. Any critical or high issue is "not yet", any medium is "nearly", and nothing above low is "ready". Knowledge: off is "not yet"; on with no articles is "not yet"; fewer than 20 published articles, or `svc.knowledge-current` raised, is "nearly"; otherwise "ready". Platform: generative AI off is "not yet"; on without Agentforce is "nearly"; Agentforce on is "ready". Data Cloud and the agents are stated, not judged. Actions: nothing to call is "not yet"; fewer than half the autolaunched flows described, or `aut.undocumented-automation` raised, is "nearly"; otherwise "ready". Org shape: more than 33% of automation in old tools is "not yet"; more than 10%, or a high or critical issue among the old-tool and governor-limit checks, is "nearly"; otherwise "ready". A pillar with nothing measured is "not measurable", with the reason, and in a set-up review the data pillar says the data is part of the full check | Every verdict rests on a check or a figure the report already shows and links to. Claude words only the section's opening, and its brief forbids changing a verdict or adding a figure (rule 1) |
| 240 | The working lines are our judgement: 20 published articles, and 10% and 33% of automation in old tools. A test reads the worker's sharing code and fails on any domain, group, anyone or wider role. When: never before release (rule 10). The documents job shares the folder once it's made (253), and the console queues a `{ kind: "share", runId }` job, ids only, when it changes the readers of a released run | One list means the microsite and the files can't disagree about who sees the report: adding a reader gives both, and taking one off takes both. The files hold what the microsite shows and the action lists, which name records, so anyone fit to read one is fit to read the other, and a consultant has one place to manage. A separate list would be a second place to forget someone |
| 252 | Each share is recorded in the console's `HealthCheckDriveShare` (blackcloud-next `migrations/20261117_health_check_drive_share.sql`), one row per run, folder and address: the permission's id, the state (shared, removed or failed), when it was shared and removed, why it was removed (reader removed, folder changed, retention) and Drive's reason for a failure, never a token. Only the worker writes it, with `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT ("runId", "folderId", "email") DO UPDATE`. The worker reads the readers and the shares together (`loadShareJob`). Without the share table (42P01), nothing can be recorded, so nothing is shared. Logs give counts only, never an address | The console and the microsite act on what Drive was actually told, not on who is on the list: someone whose share failed doesn't get a link that would ask them to request access |
| 253 | Sharing follows the list. A share job, the documents job after a make, and the retention sweep all call one function, `syncShares`: it takes access away first from anyone no longer on the list, or on a folder that's no longer the run's, then shares with anyone on the list who doesn't have it, one at a time, because Drive doesn't take changes to one folder's permissions at once. A refusal is recorded on its row as failed, with Drive's reason, and tried again the next time. Access that can't be taken away stays recorded as shared, with the reason. A share job holds the run's lock, so it never runs alongside a documents job moving that run's folder. A failed share fails the job only on its last delivery. A sharing problem never fails the documents | A list that changes while something's failing has to settle on its own, without a person working out what Drive was last told |
| 254 | At 12 months, the retention sweep (98) takes everyone's Drive access away before the run folder goes, whether or not that folder is still on the worker. While anyone still has access because Drive refused to take it away, the run folder is kept, logged as `access_kept`, and the next sweep tries again, so the record of who has the files isn't lost before they've lost them. A dry run takes nothing away and says how many would lose it. The Drive files themselves aren't deleted here: they're Black Cloud's own record | Decision 16, and the microsite's 12 months (21) |
| 255 | The Drive scope doesn't widen. The worker already asks for `https://www.googleapis.com/auth/drive`, and sharing needs nothing more. `drive.file` would be narrower, but Google's guide doesn't say whether it reaches a folder the app didn't create, and the worker creates the client folders inside "Health checks", which a person made. That couldn't be checked without real credentials, so the scope stays as it is, and QUESTIONS.md asks for the check. Sharing does need two Drive settings that no scope gives: in a shared drive, only a Manager can share a folder, or a Content manager where the drive allows it (`sharingFoldersRequiresOrganizerPermission` off), and the shared drive must allow people outside the organisation | Narrowing a scope that works, without a way to test it, risks the documents for nothing. Google's sharing guide (developers.google.com/workspace/drive/api/guides/manage-sharing), read on 6 November 2026, gives the shared-drive roles |
| 256 | Considered and not done: expiring each share at 12 months through the permission's own `expirationTime`. Google allows it for a folder only with the reader role, but its guide doesn't say whether it works in a shared drive, so the sweep (254) does it instead | A share that silently failed to be made because of an expiry rule would leave a reader with no files and no reason recorded |

Its cloud is `pardot`. It reads only the Salesforce side: the fields the managed package (namespace `pi`) puts on Lead, and the org's campaign settings. Each `pi__` field the checks read (`pi__url__c`, `pi__score__c`, `pi__grade__c`) is checked against the org's own Lead describe. The cost is one retrieve, one batch, and one page per 2,000 open leads | Counts give the shares without reading every lead. Only the open ones are read row by row, because only they can be acted on |

On, but not set to use Account Engagement's influence models, is low. On, with the models allowed or the setting not returned, passes. Its figure is those leads at the org's own return per lead (`lead.won_per_lead`), one-off, with the caveat that leads left this long often return less. | 311 | Twelve months after a run's last release, the share sync wants nobody, whatever started it: a share job after a reader is added, or a documents make. It reads the release date with the run's readers (`ShareJobInput.releasedAt`, the same `MAX("releasedAt")` the retention sweep uses) and takes anyone's access away as `retention`. A run with no release date we can read is shared as before, as the sweep leaves it alone | Brief AO review. The sweep took everyone's access away at 12 months, but nothing stopped the next reader change or documents make from sharing the folder again, and the microsite can outlive the 12 months when its documents were made again late |
| 312 | A share recorded as failed may still give access: Drive can make the permission and the answer be lost on the way back, which leaves a row with no permission id. When such a row's person comes off the list, or the folder changes, or the 12 months are up, the sync looks for their reader permission on the folder (`FolderSharing.findReader`, a read-only `permissions.list` that matches a user reader by address) and takes it away if it's there, then closes the row as removed. If the look-up fails, the row stays failed with "Couldn't check whether Drive shared it", is counted as failed so the job retries, and the retention sweep keeps the run folder, as for a removal that failed. `findReader` returns only a `type: "user"`, `role: "reader"` permission, so nothing else of that address's is ever taken away | Brief AO review. Removal and retention only looked at rows recorded as shared, so a share whose answer was lost was never taken away |
| 313 | A documents job brings the folder's sharing in line with the readers however it ends, failed included, not only once the documents are in Drive. A share job that finds the run held (a documents make takes minutes) is retried twice over about 90 seconds and then given up, so the documents job, which held the run, applies the change itself. A failure that will be retried doesn't share yet: the next attempt does | Brief AO review. A reader taken off while documents were being made kept the folder when the make then failed, because their share job had given up and the documents job only shared on success |

It fails if the engine's resident peak is over `--limit-mb`, 2,500 by default. `--diagnose`, with `node --expose-gc`, collects garbage at each step's end to show what each step's output keeps. `npm run scale` runs with Node's default heap settings, as a worker would | Brief AS. In one process, the generated org's 1.6 GB hid the engine's own figure |

| 345 | Checkpoints are written and read in pieces (`src/engine/checkpoint.ts`), never as one string. The file is still JSON, so anything that parses it whole still can, but it's compact, and an array of 1,000 items or more has an item to a line. The writer emits it as it goes. The reader rebuilds it a line at a time, from 4 MB chunks cut at newline bytes, and swaps the long arrays back in as it parses. The partial checkpoints of a keyset read are read a line at a time too. A checkpoint from before, with two-space indents, is read whole, so a run started before this resumes. The benchmark export and the test harness read through the same reader | Brief AS. At about twice the size it would pass V8's limit on a string's length, and a run couldn't be written or resumed (scale.md). The extraction peak went from 1.65 GB to about 1.0 GB, the run folder from 583 MB to 438 MB, and the fingerprints are unchanged |
| 346 | Each step's output is walked once when it finishes, and when its checkpoint is read on resume, and each repeated value is swapped for the first copy seen (`shareRepeatedValues`). The table that finds the copies is dropped when the walk ends. Values are unchanged, so no rule can tell, and the fingerprints are the same | Brief AS. JSON.parse makes a new string for every value it reads, so a big org's 500,000 stage changes held their stage names, dates and opportunity ids 500,000 times over. What the extraction keeps for analysis went from about 600 MB to 470 MB |

| 371 | At most one heavy job, a run or a documents make, runs at a time in each worker process, and connect and share jobs never wait behind one. The console's queue, `health-check-jobs`, is worked as before, `HC_WORKER_CONCURRENCY` at a time, but a run or documents job taken from it is handed to a second queue, `health-check-heavy`, with the console's own options and id, and the console's job is done. A worker in the same process takes the heavy queue one job at a time. The console's code doesn't change: it still enqueues by name to `health-check-jobs`, and reads progress from the database, never from the queue | Brief AV. Two runs at once came to 2.05 GB (decision 347), and with one slot a connect job, whose authorisation code lasts minutes, could wait behind a 25-minute run. A semaphore inside one queue's slots was ruled out: a waiting heavy job would hold a slot, and a connect could still wait behind it. BullMQ's open-source groups and queue-wide concurrency can't tell jobs apart by name. A second worker process would carry a heap of its own |
| 372 | A heavy job waiting its turn is in the heavy queue, waiting, not active. So it holds no lock and can't time out, and no attempt is counted until it starts, so a run held behind another starts on its first attempt. The heavy job carries the console's attempts, backoff, priority and removal options, so its retries, its last attempt and its order among waiting jobs work as they did: documents (priority 5) before runs (10). It keeps the console's job id, so if the console's queue hands the same job over twice, say after a crash before it was marked done, BullMQ keeps the one it has and adds nothing | Brief AV: a waiting heavy job mustn't time out, be lost, or be retried as a failure |
| 373 | The heavy queue's lock lasts 10 minutes (`HEAVY_LOCK_MS`), against BullMQ's 30 seconds. Analysis runs for tens of seconds without yielding (about 20 at 500 users), during which the lock can't be renewed. With the default, a healthy run could be taken for a dead one and handed out a second time. Nothing holds the gate but BullMQ's own active job: if the process dies, the job's lock lapses, and BullMQ's stalled-job check puts it back to waiting within 10 minutes. A restarted worker starts with its heavy slot free, and the run resumes from its checkpoints. BullMQ fails a job that stalls a second time, as it already did | Brief AV: a crash while holding the gate mustn't leave it held. The 10 minutes is the cost: a job whose worker died waits that long before it's picked up again |

| 396 | A heavy job BullMQ gives up on is recorded, not left in progress for ever. In BullMQ 5.81 (worker.js `getNextJob`, moveStalledJobsToWait-9.lua), a job whose process died is put back once its lock lapses; past `maxStalledCount` it's marked "job stalled more than allowable limit" and failed without its handler running, so only the `failed` event knows, and the gate only logged it. The run stayed at "extracting" or "analysing", and documents at "making", with nobody told. Now `startQueueWorker` takes `onLost`, which the worker sets to `lostRecorder`: a run is recorded as failed and documents as not made, with "The worker stopped part-way more than once, by a restart or by running out of memory, so this was given up. Queue it again: a run picks up from where it got to." And the heavy worker allows two stalls (`HEAVY_MAX_STALLS`, BullMQ's default is 1), since a run resumes from its checkpoints: two deploys during one long run shouldn't lose it | The night 3 review of the heavy gate. Render restarts the worker on every deploy, and SIGTERM waits for the run in hand only until Render's grace period ends. A run of 25 minutes or more over a night of merges can be killed twice, and a 500-user org that runs out of memory is killed every time |
| 397 | A checkpoint cut short never reads as complete. `writeCheckpoint` writes beside the checkpoint (`<name>.<pid>.writing`) and renames it over once whole, so a write that stops part-way leaves the last good one, and a failed write removes its temp file. `readCheckpoint` refuses a file that ends inside a spilled list: a top-level list of 1,000 items or more cut at a line end used to parse as a shorter list, since its skeleton was still valid JSON. And a step's page log (`.partial.jsonl`) is cut back to its last whole line before it's read: half a line from a crash mid-append made every later resume throw, and the next append would have run on from it. The keyset read then carries on from the last whole row | The night 3 review of decision 345. Resume was already protected by ordering (a step is "done" only after its checkpoint is written), and every extractor returns an object, which a cut file can't parse as; so the list case wasn't reachable, but nothing in the reader said so. | 457 | What an Apex class or trigger touches is a reading of its text, and says only what the text can say. With comments removed and every string's contents blanked: the objects named after `FROM` in its inline queries (the outermost object of each query, and the object of a query nested in a `WHERE`; a child relationship in a `SELECT` isn't an object and isn't kept), sorted, at most 50 with the full count; the kinds of change it makes (insert, update, upsert, delete, undelete, merge, convert lead, as statements or `Database` methods); and whether it builds a query at run time (`Database.query` and its kin), in which case the handbook says the objects that query reads aren't known. The object a change is made to isn't kept: the statement names a variable, and text alone can't say what the variable holds. A body Salesforce hides gives "couldn't be read" for that class, not an empty list. A query built at run time is any `Database` method whose name starts `query`, `countQuery`, `getQueryLocator` or `getCursor` (so every `WithBinds` form), and `Search.query`; `getQueryLocator` given an inline query reads that query's object instead. It also says whether the code searches (an inline `[FIND`, `Search.query` or `Search.find`): a search isn't a query, and the objects it reads aren't kept. Nothing inside an inline query's or search's brackets is read as a change, so `UPDATE TRACKING` and `FOR UPDATE` aren't an update; the scan's count of changes in loops has the same fix. Like the scan beside it, the reading errs towards missing a case rather than inventing one | Rule 5: a guess at which object `update records;` changes would be an estimate printed as a fact. Decision 480 ended the case subquery the replies and the Email channel count read through, but left the open-case read (`IsClosed = false`) with no end and the emails and article links bounded only by their case's dates. Now the open-case read adds `CreatedDate <=` the run's end, the two `EmailMessage` reads add `MessageDate <=` it and the `CaseArticle` read adds `CreatedDate <=` it. No step's version goes up, as in 480. What 480 promises is narrower than its row says: reads with a window give what they would have at the start; a read of how things stand with no window (open leads, contacts, reports, users, won accounts) is still as it stood when it was read, and a record changed or closed in between can't be wound back. As built: the synthetic org's generator already held two replies dated after its own date, on email cases opened on its last day, and the old read took them in; with the end, `svc.first-reply-email` on the recorded synthetic org reads 369 answered and 37 of 406 with no reply yet (was 371 and 35), late 62 (was 63); the median and the outcome don't move. Left as they are, by name in the test: the count of cases by owner that guards deactivating a user (a case made since still belongs to them, and ending it would hide work from a guard) and the all-time side of field usage (the rule tests the two fill rates apart) | The adversary on decision 480's change, both confirmed by the verifier. A figure built from two reads needs both to stop at the same moment, or it compares cases from one day with replies from another |

The placeholder is one constant, and the handbook treats a token restored to it as not looked up. An id saved as the placeholder by an earlier analysis keeps it even when Salesforce later gives the name, because a token's name is never rewritten: that line says we couldn't look them up, which is true of that run's map | The adversary on decision 473's change, confirmed by the verifier; the builder had flagged it too. | 465 | The opportunities read stops at the run's own date, the read decision 480 left out. Three parts. (1) The scope keeps its OR and gains `AND CreatedDate <=` the run's start rounded up to its second (`soqlUpTo`), so a deal made after the run's date is not read; the `OpportunityHistory` read inherits that through its subquery, and the next-steps read (`next-steps`, set against the open deals by `cur.missing-next-steps`) gains the same end. (2) A stage, amount or close date changed after the run's date is wound back from the deal's own history. The history read keeps no end of its own in the query, because the later rows are how the engine knows a deal changed: in code, a history row made after the run's end is dropped, and a deal that had one takes `StageName`, `Amount` and `CloseDate` from its last row at or before the end, and `IsClosed` and `IsWon` from the stages read for that stage. (3) A deal with no history row after the end is left exactly as read, so a fresh run, where nothing has happened since, gives every figure it gave before: this is the test that holds the change. Where a deal can't be wound back (it has a later row but none at or before the end, the stages read isn't ok, or the earlier stage isn't in it), the deal and its history are kept as read, which is what every deal was before this change, and the step counts them. What history can't wind back stays as read and is said here: the owner, `LastActivityDate`, type, source and loss reason, a deal deleted since, and a deal closed since that the next-steps read (open deals as they stand) no longer lists, which shows as wound back to open with no next step. No step's version goes up: the shape kept doesn't change, so a run in flight keeps what it has read (as in 480). The reads with no window that sit beside deals (forecast facts, won accounts, ownership counts) are as they stood when read, as decision 481 says | Decision 480 named this as its own change because it moves which deals every pipeline figure counts. Ending the deals but not winding them back would have been worse than leaving it: a deal moved since would carry today's stage beside a history cut at the run's date, and "days in stage" reads the two together. The history holds exactly the three fields the pipeline figures turn on, and a row for every change to them |

| 497 | Record-type use, the data model check decision 205 left for later: `dq.record-type-use`, in data quality, from one new step, `record-type-use` (`records: true`, so a set-up review never runs it). The step reads three things, all afresh every time it runs and all ending at the run's own date: the org's record types from the data API (`SELECT Id, DeveloperName, Name, SobjectType, IsActive, NamespacePrefix, CreatedDate FROM RecordType WHERE CreatedDate <= <run date>`), and for each object with an active record type that isn't packaged, up to 25 objects with the most active types first, two aggregate counts by `RecordTypeId`: every record made by the run's date, and those made in the 12 months before it. Three calls at most. The rule judges an active, unpackaged record type made more than 90 days before the run, on an object with at least 20 records made in the last 12 months, and reports two kinds apart: types with no records at all, and types with records but none made in the last 12 months. One finding for the org, low severity, medium confidence, owner the Salesforce admin, no figure (not sized). It is declared as a deactivate recommendation with one guard per record type, `record-type-references`, which is always unknown for now, so every finding prints as "review" and never as "deactivate". Record types are named by label (decision 412) with `Object.DeveloperName` in the evidence. Not judged, and counted in the exclusions: packaged types, types made in the last 90 days, objects under the 20, objects past the 25, and any object whose count Salesforce refused or cut short | A record type with nothing on it is clutter on every picklist and page-layout assignment, and decision 205 named this as the next data model check. The 90 days and the 20 records are our judgement: a new type hasn't had time to be used, and on an object nobody adds to every type would read as unused, which is a finding about the object, not its types. "None made in 12 months" is said as exactly that, not as "unused": a Partner account type can be in daily use with no new partner this year. The guard is unknown because nothing we read says which profiles default to a type or which flows, Apex and integrations name it, and unknown blocks (rule 3); it becomes a real check only when profiles' record-type settings are read. No figure, because the only one to hand would be a guess at upkeep hours.
