| 498 | Record-type use, corrected after review (adds to 497). (1) **Only objects whose every record the connected user can see are counted.** The step's first read becomes the connected user's own access: whether any permission set or profile they hold gives View All Data, and which objects they hold View All Records on. The run is told who connected (the user id kept with the token; where a connection has only a username, as the `sf` route does, the step looks the id up from it), with no new route and nothing new on the client; the evidence quotes the access queries with a placeholder for the user, never the id or username. A run that doesn't know who connected judges nothing. An object without either isn't counted at all and is listed as "Objects where the connected user can't see every record". If that access can't be read, nothing is judged and the finding is not measurable. (2) **Task and Event are never counted**, and the exclusions say so. (3) **A whole count call that fails is "refused" only for the codes the engine already treats as Salesforce's answer** (`isUnavailable`); anything else (a 502 after the retries, a session that stays expired) stops the step, as every other batch read does, so it is read again on resume. One object's count failing inside a call that came back is that object refused, whatever the code, as the ownership counts have it: it costs that object's types and says so, where stopping would fail a whole health check on one odd object. (4) **The engine keeps a finding's own reason for having no figure when its guards block it**: `applyGuards` replaces the value with "Not counted as a saving, because not every guard passed" only when the rule had given a figure, or marks the finding as one it sizes as a saving (`sizesASaving`: unused custom fields' hours, the clear users' seats), whose own reason, a time or price not agreed, would read on a blocked finding as though agreeing one gave a figure. (5) Said as built: every group of records counts towards the object's 20, the one with no type and any id the list doesn't hold (Master's, or a type deleted since), and none of those is judged as a type; an org with no active types of its own older than 90 days passes with nothing to segment; the exclusion counts cover only objects holding a type we would otherwise judge; and the step makes six calls at most (one to look the user up where only a username is known, two for the access, one for the list unless an org has over 2,000 record types, two for the counts) | (1) A count sees only the records the connected user's sharing lets them see, and anyone can connect (decision 151): on Barometer Read Only the user has View All Records on six objects and nothing else (decision 59), so a custom object with Read added, or any object for an ordinary user, would count some records, pass the 20, and print a type in daily use as having "no records at all". Nothing in a count says it was cut by sharing, so the only safe test is the permission itself, read in the same step and afresh. The cost is coverage: on the read-only route only the six objects are judged, which is where most record types are. (2) A plain query leaves out activities archived after a year (decision 35) and View All doesn't exist for them on the read-only route, so a task type last used 14 months ago would read as never used; `queryAll` stays off the allowlist. (3) The first build saved a temporary failure as a finished step saying Salesforce had refused, which a resume would never read again. (4) The sentence was true of the two rules that had it, which both size a saving; this finding never has one, and the sentence implied one was waiting. All four were found by the adversary before merge |
| 499 | Record-type use, second correction (adds to 497 and 498). (1) **The objects a plain count can't see whole are a named list, never counted whatever the user's access**: Task and Event (archived activities), ContentVersion and ContentDocument (a user with View All Data still sees only the files they own or share, unless they also hold Query All Files), and every Knowledge article version object (a name ending `__kav`, where a plain query returns versions by publish status). One exclusion line names them and says why. The access evidence no longer says a user with View All Data "sees every record in the org": it says they see every record on the objects we count. (2) **A permission set that needs activating in the session doesn't count as access**: both access reads leave out sets with `HasActivationRequired`. (3) **The id the step looks up from a username is hidden like the one it is given**, in every reason and fault | All three were found by the second adversary pass before merge. (1) Decision 498 treated View All Data as proof of seeing everything; it isn't on files, and the first build's Task and Event were two cases of the same thing, so they become one list with one reason. We can't prove the list complete, so it holds what Salesforce documents and QUESTIONS.md asks a real org about the rest; an object left off it errs only where View All really does show everything. Query All Files isn't read, to keep the access reads to two: files are simply never counted. (2) A session-based set shows as assigned while the API session has none of its permissions, so the count would run under ordinary sharing and a zero would be printed as "no records at all". (3) The hider was built before the lookup, so on the `sf` route a Salesforce reason quoting the query carried the user's id into the checkpoint, the finding and run.json (reproduced); rule 7 |
| 500 | Storage growth, the half of roadmap 3.2 that decision 204 left: **a re-check sets its own storage reading beside the earlier health check's.** Nothing new is read from Salesforce and no step, rule or finding changes: it is worked out when the documents are made, in the progress comparison (`src/progress/compare.ts`), from the `tech.storage-use` finding each run already keeps in `findings.json` (the facts `DataStorageMBUsed`, `DataStorageMBMax`, `FileStorageMBUsed`, `FileStorageMBMax`, as each report used them, a consultant's correction included). (1) **What is compared.** A storage kind is compared only when both runs' finding is an issue or a pass, both were made by a rule version on a named list of versions that read storage the same way (1 and 2 today; a test fails when the rule's version isn't on the list, so whoever raises it decides), and both hold that kind's used and allowance figures as whole numbers of MB, allowance above 0. (2) **The days between** are whole days between the moments the two runs' `api-limits` step finished (`finishedAt` in each `run.json`), which is when each reading was taken, not the runs' dates: a run stopped and resumed days later reads its limits when it connects again. No such time on either side, or the later one not after the earlier, and nothing is compared. (3) **What it prints**, one sentence a kind, on the progress page of both decks, on the microsite's progress page and in the raw notes' "Since your last health check": "Data storage: 8,940 of 10,240 MB used then, 9,410 of 10,240 MB now, up 470 MB in 118 days (an average of 119 MB every 30 days)." Down reads "down 470 MB in 118 days" and no change "the same as 118 days ago", neither with an average; the average is given only for a rise over 30 days or more, rounded to a whole MB. (4) **When it can't be compared** it says so in one sentence, each true of every case it covers: the earlier run has no storage finding, "Your last health check didn't read storage, so we can't say how it has changed."; either finding not measurable, "Storage couldn't be read this time, so we can't say how it has changed since your last health check." or "…couldn't be read last time…"; anything else under (1) or (2), or no kind held by both, "We can't set the two health checks' storage readings side by side, so we don't compare them." A re-check with no storage finding of its own (technical health wasn't in it) prints nothing about storage. (5) **Not printed:** a date the allowance would be reached, a cost, and anything in the pulse result or the one-pager. Every figure printed is in the number check's list for the progress section | Decision 204 was right that the org keeps no history, but a re-check holds two readings of ours, so the change between them is measured, not estimated (rule 5). The fact keys, how "used" is worked out (allowance less what remains, never below 0) and the MB unit are the same in versions 1 and 2 of the rule (df1c78c, b1e4a67: version 2 changed only how the largest objects are named), which is why both are on the list. The step's finish time is used because the limits are those of the connection, and a reused checkpoint keeps the first connection's. | 511 | A consultant's override is carried into a finding only where that is certain, and worked out from the finding as the run read it (replaces 506, which was never merged, and closes what 509 left open by another route than the number check). For each figure of a finding that has overrides (a numeric fact, or the sized amount), only two values count: the value **as read** (the stored finding's) and the **last** override's new value; the rows between are history for the raw notes. (1) Last value equal to the value as read: nothing is done, so overriding a figure back always undoes it, however many rows there are. (2) The first override's `previousValue` isn't the value as read (the findings were delivered again after the override): refused. (3) Otherwise the value as read is looked for, plain and with en-GB separators, under 506's boundaries (never inside a longer number), in **every text of the finding a document prints**: title, summary, recommendation, each line of the money working (`value.assumptions`), `value.period`, `value.reason`, `effort.note`, owner, each dependency, `notMeasurableReason`, `segmentExemptReason` and each guard's detail, with pseudonym tokens taken out first so a token's digits are never a figure. **Found exactly once in all of them, and no other fact or the sized amount of the finding has the same value as read: that one place is rewritten, in the form found ("10,500" to "9,800"), and the fact changes.** Found twice or more, or once while another figure shares the value: refused. Found nowhere: the fact changes and no text does, unless a text holds a number, other than the value itself, within 5% of the value or of the value multiplied or divided by 100, 1,000 or 1,000,000 (the figure printed rounded, as a percentage, or in thousands or millions), and then it is refused. (4) Several figures of one finding are each judged against the finding as read and rewritten in one pass, so a new value is never matched again; if any figure of a finding is refused the whole finding stays as read. (5) An override that names a finding, metric or fact the run doesn't hold changes nothing, as before, and no longer rewrites text. Metric overrides are as before (a metric has no text of its own). Admitted limits, each planted in the tests with what the customer then reads: a figure found once is taken to be the fact, so a constant that equals an unprinted fact would be rewritten ("the first 2 open stages" with an unprinted fact of 2); a figure printed in a form the 5% test doesn't see (days as weeks) stays; a second figure worked out from the overridden one (a percentage beside its count, the sized amount beside its deals) stays until it is overridden too | Measured on the synthetic run (session 22, 103 findings, 319 numeric facts, 24 sized amounts): 33 cases of two figures of one finding sharing a value (`cur.coverage` quota and remaining, four findings' `annualSaving` and sized amount); 41 figures occurring more than once in the title and summary, some of them constants ("1 is filled on under 1%", "Invoice has 2; Opportunity has 2" beside `objects` = 2); 45 figures repeated in the money working, 5 in the recommendation and 1 in a guard's detail, none of which the old swap touched; 80 figures in no text at all. A rewrite by value can't tell these apart, and session 21's adversary showed each going wrong on a real finding. Working from the finding as read removes the chain faults (an override to a value already printed, then a later one) and gives the console, which has no undo (`lib/health-check/repository.ts:143-144` has only list and add), a way back that always works. A wrong figure in front of a customer is worse than a correction we refuse, so the rule is strict and will refuse many small counts; the builder measures how many of the overridable figures fall each way and the number goes to Coby. The certain fix for the rest is for rules to say which printed figure is which fact, which is a change to every rule and not this lane |

(2) Both clients are built with everything the 1.4.0 SDK would otherwise take from the environment given outright: the credential (which alone keeps `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` and `ANTHROPIC_AUTH_TOKEN` out), `baseURL` `https://api.anthropic.com`, `logLevel` off, no webhook key, no tracing. `ANTHROPIC_CUSTOM_HEADERS` can't be shut out by an option and is admitted: nothing sets it, and the wiring note says not to. (3) A failed sign-in is thrown on as an error of our own whose message is fixed and which carries nothing of the SDK's error; the server logs one line with the exchange's status code and request id and nothing else. Its install moved `zod` from 4.6.5 to 4.6.7 under the whole site and brought `execa`. 552's "with no file and no write to the environment" was written from the docs' account of the function, not its source. The header is where Vercel puts a function's token and the variable is where a build or `vercel env pull` puts it; the package's own plain reader takes the same two. Read through `next/headers` it needs no undocumented symbol; that the header is there inside a server action's later work is not verified and can't be here (Coby's first call). (2) From `client.mjs:102-185` of the alias: the base URL, log level, webhook key and tracing each fall back to a variable unless given. (3) The SDK's exchange error keeps a non-JSON body whole and `error_description` as sent (`lib/credentials/types.mjs:65-98`), so a vendor's echo of the assertion would sit in its message; the console's catches log only an error's name today (`actions.ts:812-815`, `:391-396`), and with the SDK's own logging off nothing else can print it. | 603 | The org's inventory reads fit the allowlist as it stands, so no route or query shape is added (engine, Salesforce access; high risk; Coby's approval of 20 November 2026 to add read routes went unused). Every read is a single SELECT through REST or Tooling query, a composite batch of REST queries, an sObject describe, or the `/limits` read the client already makes free at connect: the client keeps every top-level limit from that read in `allLimitsAtConnect`, a getter that sends nothing (named in `CLIENT_METHODS`). The reads (`src/extract/inventory.ts`): field fill rates by `SELECT COUNT(Id) n, COUNT(<field>) f0 … FROM <object>` (604); packages by Tooling `InstalledSubscriberPackage` with `SubscriberPackage.Name`, `.NamespacePrefix` and `SubscriberPackageVersion.Name`, `.MajorVersion`, `.MinorVersion`, `.PatchVersion`, `.BuildNumber`, `.IsDeprecated`, `.IsManaged`, read again without the two flags if Salesforce refuses them, beside `PackageLicense`'s seats; connected apps by `ConnectedApplication` (Name, OptionsAllowAdminApprovedUsersOnly) and `OauthToken` (AppName, UserId, CreatedDate, LastUsedDate, UseCount, at most 50,000 rows), each app's users counted while the step holds the tokens and never kept; risky permissions by describing `PermissionSet`, then one `PermissionSet` read of every profile's and set's grant of Modify All Data, View All Data, Author Apex, Customize Application, Manage Users, Manage Profiles and Permission Sets, Manage Sharing, Manage Password Policies, Manage Login Access Policies, Edit Read Only Fields, Export Reports, Weekly Data Export, API Enabled, Approve Uninstalled Connected Apps and Modify Metadata, with active holders counted through `PermissionSetAssignment` and permission set groups as the least-privilege checks count them. A permission whose field the describe has under neither its API name nor its label isn't checked, and the inventory names it. A step reuses what an earlier step of the run read (the record counts, licences, connected apps and assignments) and reads it itself only when that step isn't in the run | Every read here is one the engine already makes in kind, so the boundary needs no new door, and a test proves no query in the lane selects a value from a business record. Names Salesforce hasn't confirmed to us (the two package flags, the permission for uninstalled apps) are in QUESTIONS.md and fail to "not read", never to a guess |
| 604 | Field fill rates are counted only in a run that reads records, from aggregate COUNT queries alone (engine, privacy; high risk). For each object, the share of its records with a value in each field: `COUNT(Id)` and `COUNT(<field>)`, 50 fields to a query and 25 queries to a composite call, never a value. The objects are Account, Contact, Lead and Opportunity, then every object the record count resource lists with records, most first, up to 300; how many more were left out is said. Not counted, and listed by object with why: ids and checkboxes (never empty), formulas (not filled in) and any field the describe marks not aggregatable (long and rich text, multi-select picklists, encrypted fields and the like). An object whose describe or count fails is that object's `unavailable`, not the section's. The step is `records: true`, the same condition that keeps record extracts and the rules that need them out of a set-up only scope (`modulesFor`), so Essentials and the free scan never run it and their inventory's fill rates read unavailable with "A set-up only scan doesn't count records". Counting fields in a scan or Essentials run would be wider use of the customer's records than its privacy notice describes: that is Coby's question, not this lane's | A record count is an aggregate of records, so it belongs where records are already read. Each object kept as it's finished makes the step resumable at the budget (rule 9) |
| 605 | The inventory is one file, `inventory.json`, version 1, written with the run's results and dated with the run's as-of time, and one console row (engine, output and worker; high risk: the write path). Sections `fillRates`, `packages`, `connectedApps`, `riskyPermissions` (with `checked` and `notInOrg`) and `limits`, each `{ rows, notes }` or `{ unavailable: reason }`, held by the zod schema in the new `src/schemas/inventory.ts` (no existing schema file changes). Rows hold set-up names and counts only: no user id, name or email, no field value. The worker's run job turns the inventory on for every run it makes (`RunOptions.inventory`); the command line and every run made without it read and write exactly what they did, so the fingerprint of existing outputs doesn't move. A worker run's raw notes count the inventory's calls among the API calls used, which they truly are. The worker sends the parsed file (under 8 MB, no NUL) with the results, and the Postgres store writes it to `HealthCheckInventory` ("runId" primary key and foreign key with cascade, "madeAt", "inventory" JSONB) as `HealthCheckRunFacts` is written: DELETE then INSERT inside a savepoint, a missing table (42P01) rolled back to the savepoint and said once a process so the results still save, any other error rolling the whole save back. The table's SQL is for the console lane's migration. The five step ids are new for the console's step wording | One file and one row keep the picture whole for the portal and later questions, without touching a figure a report prints. On by default for the worker only because the inventory is wanted on every customer run, and the opt-in keeps every recorded output byte for byte |
| 606 | The free scan runs without our approval (console). A valid request is approved at once by "self-serve (no approval)": the run is made, and the connect link is shown on the page after submitting and emailed. Slack gets a notice (name, company, email domain; the score once they view their results) with no Approve or Decline. `HC_SCAN_APPROVAL=on` (off by default) restores the consultant's approval exactly as before. The package install step stays until the sign-in route of `orchestration/health-check/roadmap/oauth-only-findings.md` is built | Coby decided the scan is self-serve (20 November 2026); the switch is the way back if the form is abused |
| 607 | Controls in place of a person, with approval off (console): free and throwaway addresses are refused from one list (`lib/health-check/scan/email-domains.ts`); at most 3 requests per email domain in a rolling 24 hours, declined ones counted; the per-IP rate limit, honeypot, same-origin check, one open request per address and `HC_SCAN_OPEN` stay. One scan per Salesforce org every 30 days belongs in the engine's connect job, where the org id is first known, and is not built yet | Nothing else stands between a script and the form once no person approves |
| 608 | Hardening of the self-serve scan after its review, applying only when `HC_SCAN_APPROVAL` is off (console). The email carries the connect link only and never echoes the name or company; the results page is shown on the page after asking and again after connecting, never emailed, because nobody has proved the address. The per-IP submit limit fails closed: if Redis is missing or doesn't answer, the form takes nothing (`HC_SCAN_RATE_LIMIT=open` opens it for tests, and is ignored on Vercel and in production). The address check, the domain's daily count and the insert run in one transaction under `pg_advisory_xact_lock` on a hash of the email domain (the statement was checked on the Neon development branch: a second transaction waits, another domain doesn't, and it releases at the transaction's end). An open request still waiting is started when the prospect asks again, and the "already" page never claims an email went | The adversary's four findings on 606 and 607: results reaching a mistyped domain, our sending domain carrying a stranger's text, parallel submits passing both limits, and a retry told an email was sent when none was. `REDIS_URL` must be set in production before `HC_SCAN_OPEN` goes on (Coby) |
| 609 | Corrects 604 and 605 after an adversary's five findings on hc/inventory and one parked item, all accepted (engine, the inventory; high risk). (1) The inventory never fails a run. Inside the fill-rate step, any error on a count other than a budget stop (the client's own timeout, a 5xx after the retries, a network error) makes that call's objects unavailable ("The count took too long." for a timeout, otherwise Salesforce's reason) and they're kept with the finished objects, so a resume moves past them; a describe that fails that way is that object's unavailable too. A call holds at most 5 counts, not 25, and an object with more than 1,000,000 records by the record count resource is counted in calls of its own, so a slow object can't time out its neighbours. In the engine, any other error from an inventory step marks that step failed with its reason, its section reads unavailable, and the run carries on to its findings, which don't read the inventory; a later delivery tries the step again. (2) A partial view isn't shown as the whole (rule 5). Fill rates are counted only on objects the connected user sees whole, as record-type use decides it (498): View All Data, or View All Records on the object, and never Task, Event, ContentVersion, ContentDocument or a knowledge object (`isPartlyHidden`). Every other object is that object's unavailable, "The connected user can't see every record, so a count would show only part.", and when the user's access can't be read, every object is, with why. The access is record-type use's when that step ran, or read the same way. Where the record count resource and the object's own COUNT(Id) differ by more than a tenth and more than 100 records, the object is unavailable and neither figure is printed. (3) Each field's share is divided by its own query's record count, not the first query's. (4) Packages are listed by `SubscriberPackageId`, now read and kept as `packageId`, so packages without a namespace are each listed; licences still match by namespace. (5) When every OAuth token the connected user can read is their own, or the run wasn't told who connected, each app's users, uses and dates are unavailable with why ("The connected user can only see its own authorisations"), and the installed apps are still listed. (6) Each count inside the inventory's composite calls is recorded as one API call in the run's budget, and the budget is checked for all of them before the call: we couldn't show from Salesforce's documentation in this lane that a composite batch counts once, so we count what it might cost. The other steps' composite reads are left counting once a call, because changing them moves the call count every existing run records (the raw notes and the fingerprint); that part is reported to Coby, not built | The inventory is data beside the findings, so it may be incomplete but must never cost a run or show part as whole. Counting each subrequest can only overstate the inventory's spend, which is the safe side of rule 9 |
| 610 | One free scan per Salesforce org every 30 days, enforced in the worker's connect job (engine, the connect job; high risk: tokens and refusals). Straight after the code exchange gives the org id, before the tokens are saved, a connect for a run whose product is scan asks the store for the latest moment another scan run touched the same org in the last 30 days: a scan run (not this one) whose `HealthCheckRun.orgId` matches and whose `startedAt` (when the console made it) is inside the window, or a scan run (not this one) with a connect link whose `connectedOrgId` matches and whose `usedAt` is inside the window. Org ids are compared on their first 15 characters, so the 18 and 15 character forms match, and a sandbox is its own org. Found, the tokens just obtained are revoked and the link refused, as a wrong-org sign-in is: "This Salesforce org had a free Barometer Scan in the last 30 days. You can run another after <date>, or talk to us about a plan that scans every month.", the date being that latest moment plus 30 days as a UK date. A run with product barometer (or pulse) is never asked. The scan's own run is left out, so its own earlier attempts never count, and a refused attempt of another scan never counts because a refused link has no connected org and its run keeps "awaiting-connect". If the store can't answer, the tokens are revoked and the link refused with "We couldn't check whether this Salesforce org has had a free scan recently, so we disconnected it and read nothing. Start again from the link.": the code is spent by then, so a retry couldn't sign in anyway, and tokens must not outlive a link that didn't connect; the worker logs one line, "The free-scan cap couldn't be checked: SQLSTATE <code>", with the five-character SQLSTATE alone ("no code" when there is none), never the error's message, an org id or a token, so a refusal of every scan (a renamed console column, say) is never silent. A delivery that finds its own tokens stored from an earlier delivery has passed the check already and isn't asked again. No schema change. Admitted: two scans of one org connecting in the same second can both pass, and a scan that connected and then failed still counts | 607 left this cap to the engine because the org id is first known at the exchange. The console starts scans with no person approving them (606 to 608), so without it one org could be scanned over and over. Using the columns that exist keeps it free of a migration |
| 611 | The org's opportunity stages are read at connect and written to the console's `HealthCheckOrgStages`, for the calibration screen (engine, the connect job and the write path; high risk; sprint 2 item 2.7, after 602). After a link is marked used, for every product, the worker sends the engine's own stage query (`STAGES_SOQL` in `src/extract/opportunities.ts`, `SELECT ApiName, MasterLabel, IsActive, IsClosed, IsWon, SortOrder FROM OpportunityStage`) through the allowlisted client the read-only check opened, as one call under that client's budget, and writes one row per run: "runId" (primary key, foreign key to `HealthCheckRun` with cascade), "readAt" TIMESTAMP(3), "stages" JSONB, an array of `{ apiName, label, isActive, isClosed, isWon, sortOrder }` in sort order (no sort order last, then by API name), DELETE then INSERT in one transaction, so a reconnect replaces it. It never fails or delays a connect: the read and write together have 10 seconds; a refused read, a reply of another shape, a budget stop, a missing table (42P01, said once a process) or any other store error is one log line that names only the kind of failure (Salesforce's error code, or the SQLSTATE), and the connect's outcome is as before. A read that comes back after its 10 seconds writes nothing. When the read-only check couldn't open a client, the stages aren't read and the log says so. Stage names go to this table only: never a log line, the facts file or a thrown error. The table's SQL is for the console lane's migration | The calibration screen's stage list is typed by hand, so a stage nobody types is invisible, and on the first real run that left about twenty checks unmeasured (602). Only the worker holds the tokens. Reusing the check's client means no second connect and no second limits read |
| 614 | A complexity score from 0 to 100 with a band, separate from the health score (engine, a new output; normal risk: no figure a customer reads moves; sprint 2 item 2.4). It says how big and tangled the org's build is, never whether that is good or bad: Simple (0 to 24), Moderate (25 to 49), Complex (50 to 74), Very complex (75 to 100). It reads no records, only extracts every set-up only scan already makes, so a set-up only and a complete run of the same org score the same. Seven components with fixed weights summing to 100, each made of counts the engine already holds; each count has its own points and a top, and earns `points × min(count, top) ÷ top`, rounded to two places, so it saturates at its top and one enormous number can't take more than its own points. (1) Data model, 20: own custom objects (top 100, 8 points), own custom fields on custom objects and on Account, Contact, Lead and Opportunity (top 1,500, 8), active record types on the same (top 50, 4). (2) Declarative automation, 15: active record-triggered flows (top 100, 6), other active flows, not Process Builders (top 100, 4), active validation rules (top 200, 5). (3) Legacy automation, 10: active Workflow Rules (top 100, 5), active Process Builders (top 50, 5). (4) Code, 20: active Apex classes not marked @isTest (top 500, 8), active triggers (top 50, 4), characters of that code without comments, as the code-size check counts them (top 3,000,000, half Salesforce's org limit, 8). (5) Automation overlap, 10: objects running two or more of Apex triggers, record-triggered flows, Process Builders and Workflow Rules, active, each kind found as the mixed-automation check finds it, though that check raises an issue only at three (top 20, 10). (6) Integrations and packages, 15: connected apps (top 30, 5), own named credentials and active remote sites (top 30, 5), installed packages with a namespace (top 30, 5). (7) Access model, 10: custom profiles (top 50, 4), own custom permission sets (top 150, 4), roles (top 200, 2). Everything is the customer's own: installed packages' objects, fields, flows and code are left out, as the extracts already leave them. Sharing rules aren't counted, because a read-only connection can't count them (QUESTIONS.md). A component is measured only when every count in it was read whole; otherwise it's not read, with the reason (rule 5). All seven read: the score is the points' sum, rounded. One or more not read: when at least five components and 70 points of weight were read, the score is the points read × 100 ÷ the weight read, rounded, and names each component left out; fewer, and the score is not measurable and names each. The output carries the working: each count with its top and points, each component's points of its weight and its share of the points earned. No machine learning, benchmark or estimate; the tops and weights are our judgement, written here and in `src/complexity/formula.ts`, and a change to them is a new formula version. It feeds nothing: not the Barometer score, an area score, a finding, the raw notes or any printed document. It's written to the run folder as `complexity.json` (version 1, schema in the new `src/schemas/complexity.ts`, dated with the run's as-of time) on every run, and the worker sends it with the results to the console's `HealthCheckComplexity` ("runId" primary key and foreign key with cascade, "madeAt" TIMESTAMP(3), "complexity" JSONB), DELETE then INSERT in a savepoint, a missing table (42P01) said once a process and the results saved without it, as the inventory is (605). The table's SQL is for the console lane's migration. No existing schema file, metric, score or page changes, so the fingerprint doesn't move | A large customer's fix work and quote depend on how much there is to change, which the health score doesn't say. A competitor publishes a complexity score; ours must be explainable line by line, so it's a short published table of counts, tops and weights rather than a model. Saturating each count stops one huge figure (thousands of fields) deciding the band alone. A new file and table keep every existing output byte for byte |
| 615 | The three optional side tables never cost a run its results (engine, the worker's write path; high risk; corrects 324, 433, 605 and 614 after an adversary's finding on hc/complexity, accepted and widened). For `HealthCheckRunFacts`, `HealthCheckInventory` and `HealthCheckComplexity`, any error in SQLSTATE class 42 (the table or a column missing, a different type: 42P01, 42703, 42804 and the rest) or class 23 (integrity: a NOT NULL column with no default, a key or check the worker's row breaks) rolls back to that write's own savepoint, is logged once a process for each table by the table's name and the five-character SQLSTATE alone, never the error's message or any value, and the run's results still commit without that row. The facts keep their one retry without the `file` column on 42703 (433); the retry's own class 42 or 23 error is then handled the same way. Errors of any other class (a lost connection, 08; a deadlock or serialisation failure, 40; anything else) still roll the whole save back, so the delivery is tried again as before. A missing table keeps its own sentence asking for the console's migration | The console's migrations are written by hand in another repo, so a column named or typed slightly differently, or a NOT NULL column the worker doesn't fill, would fail the INSERT and, with only 42P01 caught, roll back the whole transaction and fail the run on its last delivery though nothing in the customer's results was wrong. These tables are extras beside the results; a fault in one is the console's to fix, and the log says which table and code to look at |
